Español What to know After November 3, 2026, at 11:59 pm Eastern Standard Time, all people boarding an international flight to the United States must attest (sign a statement) that they have not been present in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) in the 21 days before boarding a flight to the United States. The overall risk to the American public and travelers remains low. Travel from DRC: Travelers, including U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals, who have been in DRC within 21 days before departure will not be allowed to board flights with U.S. destinations. These travelers should plan to remain outside the United States for at least 21 days after they leave DRC. If they have been in outbreak-affected provinces in DRC, they may be subject to additional travel requirements from local authorities before they leave DRC. Travel from South Sudan or Uganda: Non-U.S. citizens/non-U.S. nationals, including U.S. lawful permanent residents (Green Card holders), who have been in South Sudan or Uganda within 21 days before departure will not be allowed to enter the United States and should plan to remain outside the United States for at least 21 days after they leave South Sudan or Uganda. U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals who have been present in Uganda or South Sudan (and not present in DRC) within 21 days before arrival in the United States will have their air travel routed through designated U.S. airports for public health entry screening. Background To reduce the risk of Ebola importation into the United States, all people boarding an international flight into or connecting through the United States must attest (sign a statement) that they have not been present in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) in the 21 days prior to boarding the flight to the United States. Travelers, including U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals, who have been in DRC within 21 days before their flight will not be allowed to board flights with U.S. destinations and should plan to remain outside the United States for at least 21 days after they leave DRC. This includes all passengers transiting through DRC on a connecting flight regardless of whether they disembark or stay on the airplane. Additionally, non-U.S. citizens/non-U.S. nationals, including U.S. lawful permanent residents (Green Card holders), who have been in South Sudan or Uganda within the 21 days before departure will not be allowed to enter the United States and should plan to remain outside the United States for at least 21 days after they leave South Sudan or Uganda. U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals who have been present in South Sudan or Uganda (and not present in DRC) within 21 days before arrival in the United States must only enter through designated U.S. airports and will undergo public health screening. These travel restrictions and rerouting apply, regardless of whether a passenger disembarks or stays on an airplane during a connection in South Sudan or Uganda. Related CDC Orders Order: Suspending the Right to Introduce Certain Persons from Countries Where a Quarantinable Communicable Disease Exists Order: Requirement for Attestation for All Airline or Other Aircraft Passengers Arriving into the United States More information for travelers Americans who need consular assistance can visit Additional Consular Information for Americans Regarding the Ebola Outbreak, or call the Department of State: From the U.S. and Canada: +1-888-407-4747 From all other locations: +1-202-501-4444 For travelers who have been outside of DRC, South Sudan, or Uganda for more than 21 days and need assistance with a public health travel restriction, please contact Ebola-Travel-HELP@hq.dhs.gov. Before travel to the U.S. Travel from DRC The United States has temporary travel restrictions for people, including U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals, who have recently been in DRC. If you have been in DRC during the previous 21 days: You will not be permitted to board a flight to the United States until 21 days after you leave DRC. This applies even if you were on an airplane that stopped at an airport in DRC regardless of whether you disembarked or stayed on the airplane. You should plan to remain outside the United States until at least 21 days after you leave DRC. IMPORTANT Travelers who have been in outbreak provinces in the DRC may have specific travel requirements from local DRC authorities. Always check entry requirements of any country you plan to visit during the 21 days after you leave DRC. Use this graphic as a reference for when you can return to the United States. View Larger After leaving the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), travelers must remain outside the United States for 21 days. They may return to the United States beginning on Day 22. Travel from South Sudan or Uganda The United States also has temporary travel restrictions for certain travelers who have recently been in South Sudan or Uganda. Specifically, non-U.S. citizens and non-U.S. nationals, including U.S. lawful permanent residents (Green Card holders), will not be allowed to enter the United States until 21 days after they leave South Sudan or Uganda and should plan to remain outside the United States until at least 21 days after they leave South Sudan or Uganda. This restriction applies even if they were on an airplane that stopped at an airport in South Sudan or Uganda regardless of whether they disembarked or stayed on the airplane. During these 21 days, U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals who have been in South Sudan or Uganda are permitted to enter the United States at designated U.S. airports. For travelers who are permitted to enter the United States: Your travel will be rerouted to a designated U.S. airport for public health screening. This requirement applies even if you were on an airplane that stopped at an airport in South Sudan or Uganda regardless of whether you disembarked or stayed on the airplane. Your airline should be able to work with you to rebook your flights, if needed. After Arrival in the U.S. U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals who have been in South Sudan or Uganda in the past 21 days and are arriving in the U.S. will have their travel redirected to one of the following U.S. airports to undergo public health screening: Washington-Dulles International Airport (IAD) outside Washington, D.C. Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta International Airport (ATL) in Atlanta, Georgia John F. Kennedy International Airport (JFK) in New York, New York NOTE: Travelers who were in DRC, South Sudan, or Uganda more than 21 days before traveling to the United States will not need to undergo public health entry screening. During entry screening, you will: Be escorted to a screening area Complete a brief questionnaire about travel history and symptoms, if any Have your temperature checked Be observed for signs of illness by CDC staff Be enrolled in automated text messages from CDC to remind you to monitor your health for 21 days after you left DRC, South Sudan, or Uganda. Some travelers will have an additional public health assessment based on their area of travel and activities while in DRC, South Sudan, or Uganda. CDC will also share travelers' contact information with state or local public health authorities, as needed, for follow-up and support. If you do not have symptoms You will receive information about monitoring your health for 21 days after leaving the affected country. Most travelers without symptoms will continue to their destination after public health screening. If you have symptoms If you have a fever or other symptoms consistent with Ebola, a CDC public health medical officer will conduct an additional evaluation. If Ebola is suspected, you will be transferred to a hospital for medical evaluation, isolation, and care. Steps to take after travel Monitor your health Monitor your health for 21 days after you leave DRC*, South Sudan, or Uganda. After you arrive in the United States, CDC will send you automated text messages reminding you to watch for symptoms and to take action if you get sick. Take your temperature If you were in DRC*, take your temperature every day and again if you feel sick. If you were in South Sudan or Uganda, take your temperature if you feel sick. *For people who have been granted an exception to travel to the United States. Watch for symptoms Watch for possible symptoms of Ebola such as fever (100.4°F/38°C or higher) or feeling feverish, headache or body aches, rash, weakness or tiredness, sore throat, diarrhea, vomiting, stomach pain, or unexplained bleeding or bruising (a late stage of illness). Follow any additional instructions from your health department Travel During Your Monitoring Period CDC recommends that you remain in the United States and avoid international travel and cruise ship travel during your 21-day monitoring period. If you are in the United States and plan to travel domestically or internationally while you are being monitored, notify your health department before you leave. What to do if you get sick If you develop any signs or symptoms that might be Ebola: Separate yourself from others (isolate). Stay in a room by yourself and, to the greatest extent possible, don't share a bathroom with others. Call your local health department (if you are in the United States) or health officials where you are located for advice about medical care. Tell the health department about your recent travel from a country with risk of Ebola and your symptoms. They can identify the best healthcare facility for you to go to, if needed. Health department staff can also help the facility prepare to provide care for you and take any health precautions needed. Follow all their instructions. Do not travel while sick. Travel only when you have been cleared by a doctor or health official. If you need urgent medical care and you can't reach your health department right away Call a healthcare provider. If you can't reach a healthcare provider, call 911. Tell them your symptoms and that you were recently in a country with risk of Ebola. Calling ahead before going to a healthcare facility helps the facility prepare for your arrival, including contacting the health department and taking any precautions needed. View five news elements
Time
The CDC issued updated travel guidance on October 30, 2026, and the restrictions apply to travelers arriving in the United States after November 3, 2026, at 11:59 pm Eastern Standard Time.
Location and population
The travel restrictions apply to individuals traveling from the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), South Sudan, and Uganda, particularly those who have been in outbreak-affected provinces in the DRC or have been in South Sudan or Uganda within 21 days before departure.
Event
The CDC implemented travel restrictions and requirements for individuals returning to the United States from DRC, South Sudan, and Uganda, including attestation, quarantine, and screening measures, based on the risk of Ebola virus disease importation.
Scale, impact and risk
The overall risk to the American public and travelers is considered low. However, travelers from DRC, South Sudan, or Uganda who have been in outbreak-affected areas may be subject to additional travel requirements and public health screening.
Response, status and uncertainty
The CDC has issued travel restrictions and screening protocols, and these measures are in place as of the publication date. The restrictions are based on the risk of Ebola virus disease importation and are subject to further updates.

埃博拉病毒 是非洲日益严峻的挑战 - 教会时报

Ebola virus a growing challenge in Africa - The Church Times
新闻简报刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 的 埃博拉病毒 暴发 已传播到四个非洲国家,这是非洲大陆地理分布最广的流行病。刚果民主共和国已报告 4,700 多人死亡和 9,800 多例病例,其中 暴发 归因于 Bundibugyo 物种,该物种缺乏经批准的 疫苗 或 治疗。尼日利亚确认了首例病例,肯尼亚报告了 1 例死亡和 7 例孤立病例。世界卫生组织 (WHO) 强调,暴发 的复杂性日益增加,而冲突、人口流离失所和人道主义援助准入受限则加剧了这种情况。一名护士被杀,另一名医护人员在居民的袭击中受重伤,居民指责他们污染了当地的水源。世界卫生组织和地方当局正在努力遏制疫情蔓延,乌干达实施了严格的边境管制和检疫措施。联合国世界粮食计划署正在向受影响地区提供粮食,撒玛利亚救援会已建立了 治疗 中心。目前情况仍在调查中,监测 和响应行动仍面临挑战。
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发 于 2026 年 5 月得到确认,并于 2026 年 10 月报道了持续传播。发布日期为 2026 年 10 月 30 日。
地点与对象
The Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) is the primary location, with cases also reported in Nigeria and Kenya. The affected population includes over 3.5 million people facing crisis-level hunger or worse in areas impacted by the Ebola virus.
事件
埃博拉病毒 暴发 已蔓延到四个非洲国家,其中刚果民主共和国经历了第二大 暴发。暴发 是由 Bundibugyo 物种引起的,该物种尚未获得批准的 疫苗 或 治疗。一名护士被杀,另一名医护人员在居民的袭击中受重伤,居民指责他们污染了当地的水源。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 已导致刚果民主共和国超过 4,700 人死亡,报告病例超过 9,800 例。在肯尼亚,有七人正在接受隔离治疗,其中一人已死亡。世界卫生组织(WHO)指出,由于冲突、人口流离失所和人道主义准入受到限制,控制这种疾病变得越来越具有挑战性。
应对、状态与不确定性
世卫组织和地方当局正在努力遏制 暴发,但努力因冲突和错误信息而受到阻碍。乌干达已对访问过刚果民主共和国的人采取隔离措施。联合国世界粮食计划署正在向受影响地区运送粮食,撒玛利亚救援会已在刚果民主共和国设立了两个 治疗 中心。目前情况仍在调查中,监测 和响应行动仍面临挑战。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola virus a growing challenge in Africa - The Church Times

News Brief
An Ebola virus outbreak in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) has spread to four African countries, marking the most geographically widespread epidemic on the continent. The DRC has reported over 4,700 deaths and more than 9,800 cases, with the outbreak attributed to the Bundibugyo species, which lacks an approved vaccine or treatment. Nigeria confirmed its first case, while Kenya reported one death and seven isolated cases. The World Health Organization (WHO) highlighted the growing complexity of the outbreak, exacerbated by conflict, population displacement, and restricted humanitarian access. A nurse was killed and another healthcare worker was severely injured in an attack by residents who accused them of contaminating a local water source. The WHO and local authorities are struggling to contain the spread, with Uganda implementing strict border controls and quarantine measures. The UN’s World Food Programme is providing food to affected regions, and Samaritan’s Purse has established treatment centres. The situation remains under investigation, with ongoing challenges in surveillance and response operations.
Original source text (English)
Sign InSubscribeView Basket Sign InSubscribeView Basket *** DEBUG START *** *** DEBUG END *** Wednesday 04 November 2026 Home UK World Comment Opinion Analysis Columnists Leader comment Letters to the editor Features Features Interviews Faith Faith features Articles of faith Theology matters In the parish Sunday’s readings Prayer for the week Faith for Holy Places The Mystery of Faith Books & Arts Book reviews New titles Film Podcast reviews Art Music Performing Arts TV Radio Regulars Gazette Cartoons Caption competition Crossword Notebook 100 years ago Quotes Holidays Links Audio & Video Podcast Video Archive Jobs Events Home UK World Comment Opinion Analysis Columnists Leader comment Letters to the editor Features Features Interviews Faith Faith features Articles of faith Theology matters In the parish Sunday’s readings Prayer for the week Faith for Holy Places The Mystery of Faith Books & Arts Book reviews New titles Film Podcast reviews Art Music Performing Arts TV Radio Regulars Gazette Cartoons Caption competition Crossword Notebook 100 years ago Quotes Holidays Links Audio & Video Podcast Video Archive Jobs Events Ebola virus a growing challenge in Africa by Rebecca Paveley 30 October 2026 More than 4,700 people have died in the Democratic Republic of Congo, as conflict hampers efforts to contain the spread of the virus Alamy A medical worker disinfects their hands at a treatment centre for Ebola-virus disease, in Bunia, this month A medical worker disinfects their hands at a treatment centre for Ebola-virus disease, in Bunia, this month 0:00 / 0:00 THE Ebola-virus epidemic in the Democratic Republic of Congo has spread to four countries in Africa, in the most geographically widespread outbreak so far on the continent. The disease is still spreading within the DRC, where more than 4,700 have died. This latest outbreak was confirmed in May. It is the 17th outbreak in the country and the second largest, with more than 9,800 cases so far. Nigeria has confirmed its first case. Reports initially said that the patient had contracted the disease in Uganda, but this has been denied by Ugandan authorities, who declared the country free of the virus in July (News 31 July). Uganda has introduced strict new border controls in the past few days. Ugandans who have visited the DRC will have to undergo 21 days of quarantine at their own expense, the country’s Minister of Health announced on Sunday. Kenya also announced its second death from the virus, and said that seven people were being treated in isolation. All those affected were within one cluster, the Kenyan Ministry of Health reported. The latest outbreak of the virus is caused by the rare Bundibugyo species of the virus, for which there is yet no approved vaccine or treatment, although vaccine trials are under way. Transmission rates in October have averaged 71 new cases each day in the DRC and cases have now been found in an eighth province. The World Health Organization (WHO) said that containing the disease was “becoming increasingly challenging”. Its latest update report said: “These developments highlight the widening international dimension and the growing complexity of the outbreak, particularly in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, where escalating insecurity, population displacement, and restricted humanitarian access threaten to further undermine surveillance and response operations.” Misinformation about the virus and its spread led to a deadly attack on a nurse at the weekend in the DRC. On social media, the director-general the WHO, Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus, condemned: “Healthcare workers must always be protected,” he said. Advertisement The nurse was killed and another healthcare worker was severely injured while responding to an alert, it is reported. Reports suggest that they were attacked by residents who accused them of contaminating a local water source. It is the latest in a series of attacks on health workers and responders. The UN’s World Food Programme is delivering food to affected regions to ensure that families “do not have to choose between following health guidance and finding food”, including supplying food to those isolating. It estimates that nearly 3.5 million people face crisis-level hunger or worse in areas affected by the Ebola virus. Tearfund’s country director for the DRC, Poppy Anguandia, said: “We are approaching more grim milestones with a likely total of 10,000 Ebola-virus-disease confirmed cases and 5,000 deaths on the horizon in the coming weeks. We’re doing everything we can to slow rates of infection and help to contain the spread of disease.” Tearfund is working with church leaders to raise awareness of measures to combat the disease, install hand-washing stations, and counter widespread misinformation. The Christian charity Samaritan’s Purse has set up two treatment centres in the DRC. The Ebola Treatment Centre in Bunia has admitted more than 760 patients and in Nyankunde, 600 patients. So far, 1,080 patients have been discharged from the two centres. Conflict is making it harder to combat the rapid spread of the disease. Tens of thousands of people have been displaced from their homes by violence. Cuts to aid from the United States and the UK have also affected healthcare systems. Nurse Leontine Kapara Acikani told Tearfund that lack of proper equipment, including personal protective equipment, was the greatest difficulty during the outbreak. “We need to have complete protective equipment when we go into the community. Ebola is contagious, and, if we are not properly protected, we can become infected. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

美国因 埃博拉病毒 实施旅行限制

US imposes travel restrictions due to Ebola virus
新闻简报2026 年 11 月 4 日,由于刚果共和国爆发埃博拉病毒 暴发,美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 对前往美国的旅客实施了额外的旅行要求。检疫局要求乘客填写强制性证明,确认他们在过去 21 天内没有前往刚果共和国。这一要求适用于所有在刚果民主共和国任何机场过境的航空旅客,无论他们是否下机。运营飞往美国航班的航空公司已发布旅行警告并调整程序以遵守 CDC 指令。建议旅客直接咨询航空公司以了解证明和文件需求。卫生部向公众保证,菲律宾仍然没有受到 埃博拉病毒 的影响,卫生当局在全球监测工作中保持警惕。
查看新闻五要素
时间
Philstar.com 主页 头条新闻 意见 国家 世界商业 体育 娱乐 生活方式 活动 其他版块 菲律宾之星 菲律宾之星 Ngayon 主页 Bansa Metro Probinsiya 意见 Palaro 演艺圈 真实的自白 Dr. Philstar.com
地点与对象
刚果共和国、美国。
事件
据检疫局报告,由于刚果共和国爆发埃博拉病毒 暴发,美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 对前往美国的旅客提出了额外的旅行要求。
规模、影响与风险
旅客必须填写一份强制性证明,确认在过去 21 天内没有前往刚果共和国。该要求适用于在刚果民主共和国任何机场过境的所有乘客,无论他们是否下机。
应对、状态与不确定性
检疫局建议旅客直接与航空公司协调有关证明和文件要求的事宜。由于 暴发,美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 实施了旅行限制,而菲律宾仍然不受 埃博拉病毒 的影响。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

US imposes travel restrictions due to Ebola virus

News Brief
On November 4, 2026, the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) imposed additional travel requirements for US-bound travelers due to an Ebola outbreak in the Republic of the Congo. The Bureau of Quarantine mandated that passengers complete a mandatory attestation confirming they have not traveled to the Republic of the Congo in the preceding 21 days. This requirement applies to all air travelers transiting through any airport in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, regardless of whether they disembark or not. Airlines operating flights to the US have issued travel advisories and adapted procedures to comply with the CDC directive. Travelers are advised to directly consult their airlines for attestation and documentation needs. The Department of Health assured the public that the Philippines remains free of the Ebola virus, with health authorities on alert amid global monitoring efforts.

Edd Gumban MANILA, Philippines — The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) has imposed additional travel requirements for US-bound travelers due to the Ebola outbreak in the Republic of the Congo, according to the Bureau of Quarantine. In an advisory, the BOQ said passengers traveling by air to the US must complete a mandatory attestation confirming that they have not traveled to the Republic of the Congo during the preceding 21 days. The requirement covers passengers who transit through any airport in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, regardless of whether they disembark from the aircraft or leave the airport premises. The BOQ said all airlines operating flights to the US have issued travel advisories and are adapting passenger procedures to comply with the CDC requirement. The quarantine bureau advised travelers to coordinate directly with their airlines regarding applicable attestation and documentation requirements. “Declare your complete travel history, including countries visited and transited within the past 21 days,” the BOQ said. The Department of Health assured the public that the Philippines remains free of the Ebola virus, saying health authorities are on alert amid global monitoring efforts. In May, the World Health Organization declared a Public Health Emergency of International Concern linked to the Bundibugyo strain of the Ebola virus in parts of Africa. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

埃博拉病毒在创纪录增长最快的 暴发 地区导致 5,300 多人死亡 - Euronews.com

Ebola kills over 5,300 in fastest-growing outbreak on record - Euronews.com
新闻简报该新闻报道重点介绍了有记录以来增长最快的埃博拉病毒 暴发,截至 2026 年 11 月 2 日,已有 5,300 多人死亡。暴发 主要影响刚果民主共和国 (DRC),乌干达和肯尼亚等邻国也报告了病例。乌干达在记录了 20 例埃博拉病例后于 2026 年 8 月宣布本国已消除埃博拉病毒,其中大部分病例来自刚果民主共和国。2026 年 10 月,肯尼亚报告了 9 例感染病例,这是该国首次出现埃博拉病例。非洲人口最多的国家尼日利亚出现了一起病例,涉及一名 42 岁的瑞士妇女,她的丈夫在尼日利亚驻阿布贾大使馆工作。暴发 给刚果民主共和国和其他受影响地区的卫生系统带来了巨大压力。卫生当局正在进行调查,实施接触者追踪,并隔离感染者以遏制传播。由于管理如此大规模的 暴发 面临挑战,这一情况仍在受到积极关注。
查看新闻五要素
时间
据报道,截至 2026 年 11 月 2 日,暴发 已造成 5,300 多人死亡,2026 年 10 月在肯尼亚和尼日利亚发生病例。事件日期为 2026 年 10 月,发布日期为 2026 年 11 月 2 日。
地点与对象
暴发 主要影响刚果民主共和国 (DRC),邻国乌干达和肯尼亚也报告了病例。非洲人口最多的国家尼日利亚曾发生一起涉及一名瑞士妇女的案件。受影响人群包括来自刚果民主共和国的个人以及与感染者有密切接触的人。
事件
埃博拉病毒病 导致 暴发 迅速增长,导致 5,300 多人死亡。暴发 得到了卫生当局的证实,包括肯尼亚和尼日利亚在内的多个国家报告了病例,涉及来自刚果民主共和国的人员。
规模、影响与风险
2026 年 10 月,暴发 导致 5,300 多人死亡,其中肯尼亚报告了 9 例感染病例,尼日利亚报告了 1 例病例。由于该疾病的迅速传播,刚果民主共和国和邻国的卫生系统面临着巨大压力。由于存在进一步 传播 的可能性以及遏制方面的挑战,风险被描述为高。
应对、状态与不确定性
刚果民主共和国和邻国的卫生当局正在积极调查和应对 暴发。已实施接触者追踪和隔离措施,但由于追踪 传播 的复杂性以及控制传播的挑战,情况仍在调查中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola kills over 5,300 in fastest-growing outbreak on record - Euronews.com

News Brief
The news report highlights the fastest-growing Ebola outbreak on record, with over 5,300 deaths reported as of November 2, 2026. The outbreak primarily affected the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), with neighboring countries like Uganda and Kenya also reporting cases. Uganda declared itself Ebola-free in August 2026 after recording 20 cases, mostly among individuals who had traveled from the DRC. Kenya reported nine infections in October 2026, marking the first time the country had seen an Ebola case. Nigeria, Africa’s most populous nation, had a single case involving a 42-year-old Swiss woman whose husband worked at the country’s embassy in Abuja. The outbreak has placed significant strain on the health systems of the DRC and other affected regions. Health authorities are conducting investigations, implementing contact tracing, and isolating infected individuals to contain the spread. The situation remains under active scrutiny due to the challenges in managing such a large-scale outbreak.

Africa's latest Ebola outbreak has killed nearly 5,400 people, the vast majority of them in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), according to the continent's top health agency. ADVERTISEMENT ADVERTISEMENT Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention (Africa CDC) told the AFP on Monday that the outbreak, which has spread to other countries on the continent, has killed 5,376 people – 5,372 in the DRC and two each in Kenya and Uganda. The outbreak, which began in the DRC in May, has infected more than 10,000 people, with an over 50% fatality rate, according to the country’s latest data from 31 October, making it the fastest-growing and most widespread Ebola epidemic on record. Neighbouring Uganda recorded 20 cases, almost all involving people who had travelled from the DRC, before being declared Ebola-free in August. In October, Kenya recorded nine infections, marking the first time the country had reported an Ebola case. That same month, Nigeria, Africa’s most populous country, reported an Ebola case involving a 42-year-old Swiss woman whose husband works at his country’s embassy in Abuja. The couple had travelled to Uganda before returning home. The outbreak is caused by the rare Bundibugyo virus, which has no approved vaccine or treatment. The virus is transmitted through contact with bodily fluids and causes haemorrhagic fever. In recent weeks, clinical trials of the Ervebo vaccine, which has proved effective against the more common Zaire strain of Ebola, have begun. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

随着病毒继续传播,刚果埃博拉死亡人数超过 5,000 人

Congo's Ebola death toll surpasses 5,000 as virus continues to spread
新闻简报据卫生当局称,自 2026 年 5 月中旬以来,刚果民主共和国的埃博拉 暴发 已导致 5,000 多人确诊死亡。感染人数已达到10,351人,目前有1,101人正在接受治疗或正在接受隔离。此外,已有 2,788 人康复。由 Bundibugyo 病毒株引起的 暴发 病毒已传播到其他国家,包括肯尼亚和尼日利亚,病例与在刚果感染该病毒的个人有关。实际死亡人数可能更高,因为专家认为该病毒自二月份以来可能一直在未被发现的情况下传播。由于没有针对 Bundibugyo 菌株获得批准的 疫苗 或 治疗,使得遏制工作变得更加复杂。该疾病的早期症状可能会被误认为是其他疾病,例如疟疾,可能会延迟隔离和 治疗。抗病毒药物 治疗 的临床试验自 7 月以来一直在进行,研究人员正在测试 疫苗,包括现有的 Ervebo 疫苗 是否能提供针对 Bundibugyo 菌株的保护。目前情况仍在调查中,暴发 的全面影响尚不确定。
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发于2026年5月中旬被发现,截至2026年10月31日死亡人数超过5000人。发布日期为2026年10月30日。
地点与对象
刚果民主共和国,肯尼亚和尼日利亚也报告了病例。受影响人口包括超过 10,351 例感染病例和 5,026 例确诊死亡病例。
事件
据刚果卫生当局报告,自 2026 年 5 月中旬检测到 暴发 以来,刚果埃博拉死亡人数已超过 5,000 人。该事件已确认。
规模、影响与风险
已确认死亡人数超过 5,000 人,感染人数为 10,351 人。由于自二月份以来潜在的传播未被发现,实际死亡人数可能会更高。暴发 正在迅速传播,并与肯尼亚和尼日利亚的病例有关。Bundibugyo 菌株是罪魁祸首,目前还没有批准的 疫苗 或 治疗。早期症状可能会被误认为是其他疾病,从而延误隔离和 治疗。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生当局正在管理 暴发,目前有 1,101 人处于 治疗 或隔离状态。抗病毒药物 治疗的临床试验自7月份以来一直在进行,疫苗的测试也在进行中。目前情况仍在调查中,暴发 的全部范围尚不确定。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Congo's Ebola death toll surpasses 5,000 as virus continues to spread

News Brief
The Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo has resulted in over 5,000 confirmed deaths since mid-May 2026, according to health authorities. The number of infections has reached 10,351, with 1,101 individuals currently receiving treatment or undergoing quarantine. Additionally, 2,788 people have recovered from the disease. The outbreak, caused by the Bundibugyo strain, has spread to other countries, including Kenya and Nigeria, with cases linked to individuals who contracted the virus in Congo. The actual death toll may be higher, as experts believe the virus may have been spreading undetected since February. The lack of an approved vaccine or treatment for the Bundibugyo strain complicates containment efforts. Early symptoms of the disease can be mistaken for other illnesses, such as malaria, potentially delaying isolation and treatment. Clinical trials of antiviral treatments have been ongoing since July, and researchers are testing vaccines, including whether the existing Ervebo vaccine offers protection against the Bundibugyo strain. The situation remains under investigation, with uncertainty about the full impact of the outbreak.

FILE PHOTO - An Ebola nurse at the CTE ALIMA BENI Ebola Treatment Centre cares for a child suspected of having Ebola. (is associated with: «Congo's Ebola death toll surpasses 5,000 as virus continues to spread») Kitsa Musayi/dpa More than 5,000 people have died from Ebola in the Democratic Republic of the Congo since the latest outbreak of the virus was detected in mid-May, according to the country's health authorities. A total of 5,026 deaths have been confirmed, while the number of infections has risen to 10,351, the authorities said. Currently, 1,101 people are receiving treatment or undergoing quarantine. Another 2,788 people who previously contracted the disease have recovered. The actual toll could be higher, as experts believe the virus may have been spreading undetected since at least February, several months before the outbreak was officially declared on May 15. Advertisement Advertisement Advertisement Advertisement The outbreak, caused by the Bundibugyo strain of the virus, has been spreading in Congo at an alarming rate. Cases have also been reported in other countries including Kenya, linked to a Kenyan national who contracted the disease in Congo and has since died. Nigeria reported its first Ebola case on October 20, a Swiss woman who fell ill several days after returning from a weeks-long trip to Uganda. Europe has recorded one case so far: a doctor who tested positive in France in June after a humanitarian mission in eastern Congo. The doctor later recovered and was discharged from hospital. Ebola is a life-threatening disease transmitted through direct contact with infected people or their bodily fluids. It can cause severe fever, diarrhoea and bleeding. Advertisement Advertisement The current outbreak has been particularly difficult to contain because there is no vaccine or specific treatment approved for the Bundibugyo strain. Early symptoms can also be mistaken for other illnesses, such as malaria, potentially delaying the isolation and treatment of patients. Clinical trials of two antiviral treatments have been under way since July. Researchers are also testing vaccines, including whether Ervebo, an existing Ebola vaccine, offers protection against the Bundibugyo strain. Ervebo is currently approved and recommended only for protection against the Ebola virus disease caused by the Zaire strain. Time
The outbreak was detected in mid-May 2026, and the death toll surpassed 5,000 by October 31, 2026. The publication date is October 30, 2026.
Location and population
The Democratic Republic of the Congo, with cases also reported in Kenya and Nigeria. The affected population includes over 10,351 infections and 5,026 confirmed deaths.
Event
Congo's Ebola death toll surpassed 5,000 since the outbreak was detected in mid-May 2026, as reported by the country's health authorities. The event is confirmed.
Scale, impact and risk
More than 5,000 deaths have been confirmed, with 10,351 infections. The actual toll could be higher due to potential undetected spread since February. The outbreak is spreading rapidly and has been linked to cases in Kenya and Nigeria. The Bundibugyo strain is responsible, and there is no approved vaccine or treatment for it. Early symptoms may be mistaken for other illnesses, delaying isolation and treatment.
Response, status and uncertainty
Health authorities are managing the outbreak, with 1,101 people currently in treatment or quarantine. Clinical trials of antiviral treatments have been ongoing since July, and vaccine testing is also underway. The situation remains under investigation, with uncertainty about the full extent of the outbreak.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

在刚果民主共和国的埃博拉疫情中心,粮食援助和社区外展帮助抗击致命的 暴发 - 联合国世界粮食计划署

In DRC's Ebola epicentre, food aid and community outreach help fight a deadly outbreak - UN World Food Programme
新闻简报In the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), an Ebola outbreak caused by the Bundibugyo virus strain has become the world’s second largest on record, with over 10,000 cases and approximately 5,000 deaths. The outbreak, confirmed in the mining town of Mongbwalu last May, has spread to eight provinces and 66 health zones, devastating communities and exacerbating a severe hunger crisis affecting over 26 million people. The UN World Food Programme (WFP) is providing food assistance, including hundreds of thousands of hot meals to patients and healthcare workers, to help contain the outbreak and support affected families. WFP’s efforts include building treatment centers, transporting essential supplies, and offering personal protective gear and vaccines. Despite these measures, the response is hindered by ongoing conflict, limited aid access, and misinformation. The WFP emphasizes that food assistance serves as an incentive for families to seek care and comply with health measures, which are critical to interrupting the virus’s transmission. The situation remains under investigation, with the outbreak continuing to pose a significant public health threat.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该疫情于今年5月在蒙戈布卢被确认,截至2026年11月仍在持续。发布日期为2026-11-03。
地点与对象
The Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), specifically the mining town of Mongbwalu and eight Congolese provinces, including 66 health zones. The affected population includes over 26 million people facing severe hunger, with 3 million in Ebola-affected areas.
事件
去年五月在蒙布瓦卢确认了由 Bundibugyo 埃博拉病毒 菌株引起的埃博拉病毒 暴发。暴发 已导致 10,000 多例病例和约 5,000 人死亡。该事件得到了刚果民主共和国卫生当局的证实,并由联合国世界粮食计划署(粮食计划署)进行了报告。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 已导致 10,000 多人患病,其中约一半人死亡,并蔓延到 8 个省份和 66 个卫生区。它被描述为有记录以来世界第二大。其影响包括刚果民主共和国东部的严重饥饿、出入受限和持续冲突。该风险并未明确说明,但 暴发 被认为是重大公共卫生威胁。
应对、状态与不确定性
粮食计划署正在提供粮食援助、建设埃博拉 治疗 中心并支持更广泛的人道主义应对措施。暴发 仍在进行中,但响应因冲突、有限的援助、限制访问和不信任而受到阻碍。目前情况仍在调查中,需要继续努力。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

In DRC's Ebola epicentre, food aid and community outreach help fight a deadly outbreak - UN World Food Programme

News Brief
In the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), an Ebola outbreak caused by the Bundibugyo virus strain has become the world’s second largest on record, with over 10,000 cases and approximately 5,000 deaths. The outbreak, confirmed in the mining town of Mongbwalu last May, has spread to eight provinces and 66 health zones, devastating communities and exacerbating a severe hunger crisis affecting over 26 million people. The UN World Food Programme (WFP) is providing food assistance, including hundreds of thousands of hot meals to patients and healthcare workers, to help contain the outbreak and support affected families. WFP’s efforts include building treatment centers, transporting essential supplies, and offering personal protective gear and vaccines. Despite these measures, the response is hindered by ongoing conflict, limited aid access, and misinformation. The WFP emphasizes that food assistance serves as an incentive for families to seek care and comply with health measures, which are critical to interrupting the virus’s transmission. The situation remains under investigation, with the outbreak continuing to pose a significant public health threat.

Today, she is receiving WFP assistance – spreading the word about staying safe. Photo: WFP/Charly Kasereka From the eastern town of Mongbwalu, farmer and Ebola survivor Francine knows first-hand the destructive force of a disease that killed one of her children and is devastating communities across eastern Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC). The once-bustling mining town where she lives is largely shuttered. In the hardest-hit places, fresh graves are being dug. Farming is no longer possible. Neighbours and even relatives shrink from contact with Francine’s family. Merchants refuse to touch their money for fear of becoming sick. “Even I am afraid of going to the neighbours,” says Francine. “I spend my days at home and only go out to fetch water.” Since being confirmed in Mongbwalu last May, DRC’s latest Ebola outbreak has morphed into the world’s second largest on record. Caused by the rare Bundibugyo Ebola virus strain, the disease has sickened more than 10,000 people, killed roughly half of them, and spread to eight Congolese provinces and 66 health zones. Even as health officials and humanitarian workers race to contain the outbreak, ongoing conflict in eastern DRC, limited aid, restricted access, and mistrust and misinformation about the disease are hampering the response and deepening one of the world's biggest hunger crises. “Addressing hunger is a key part of the solution,” says David Stevenson, WFP Country Director in DRC. “Food assistance is an incentive for families to seek care promptly and comply with measures that protect their communities.” WFP has delivered hundreds of metric tons of food assistance to Ebola hotspots and supported the broader humanitarian response in multiple ways. Photo: WFP/Michael Castofas How food assistance helps tackle Ebola So far, WFP has delivered hundreds of metric tons of food assistance to people most impacted by Ebola, including hundreds of thousands of hot meals to patients, healthcare workers and others on the frontlines of the health emergency. The WFP-operated humanitarian air service, UNHAS, is flying critical supplies and aid workers to Ebola-affected locations. WFP is also building Ebola treatment centres, and providing storage and transport for essential supplies, including personal protective gear and vaccines, to where they’re most needed. WFP’s assistance plays a powerful role in other ways. In a country where more than 26 million people face severe hunger – including 3 million in Ebola-affected areas – our food support provides a cushion for survivors and patients, encourages less movement of people in search of food, and provides an incentive to seek and follow treatment. “If you can’t feed your family, it’s very difficult to follow health measures that are vital to interrupting Ebola’s transmission,” WFP’s Stevenson says. WFP food support includes take-home rations and hot meals for Ebola patients, medical responders and others most impacted by the virus. Photo: WFP/Michael Castofas Surviving Ebola in DRC A mother of seven, Francine was among the first in Mongbwalu to contract Ebola. Sitting in a dusty compound, she describes falling sick in April, a month before the first cases were officially confirmed. “I didn’t know it was Ebola,” she recalls. “When I left the hospital, I thought I was cured. I didn’t realize it was still in my body.” When she contracted Ebola a second time, three of her children also fell ill. “We were very sick,” she says, describing vomiting and especially diarrhoea. “By God’s grace, we are alive today.” But one of Francine’s children did not survive. Later, she fostered the baby of a couple who died from Ebola. “The family gave him to me for adoption,” she says. “With this baby that I’m taking care of more and more, we’re again a family of seven children.” Life remains difficult. Ebola, along with ongoing violence, has disrupted farming that put food on her family’s table. And like many Ebola survivors, Francine’s family faces stigma. “People on our street began to be very fearful of us,” she says. “They even forbid their children to play with mine, because they are afraid they will fall sick.” “WFP has helped us enormously,” Francine adds of WFP food assistance that includes rice, oil, pulses and salt. Workers erect a WFP-supported Ebola treatment centre in DRC's North Kivu Province. Photo: WFP/Michael Castofas Building trust in Ebola-affected communities Like Francine, Deogratias contracted Ebola early on in April, after visiting patients to offer support. He also became one of the town’s first Ebola patients to recover. Today, the teacher and community church leader credits food assistance for helping local residents to accept health workers and measures aimed at fighting the virus. “This aid has reduced our expenses,” Deogratias says, as he awaits a WFP food distribution. “I think it’s also convinced the people from Mongbwalu.” The turnaround has not been easy. “At the beginning, they thought those people came to kill us,” Deogratias says of the local reaction to health and other aid workers. “Now, they understand these workers come to help us.” Francine agrees local attitudes are changing. She and her family are careful to wash their hands. And she is warning fellow Mongbwalu residents about the dangers of contracting the virus. “My message is Ebola can kill,” she says. Slowly, she believes, local attitudes are changing – and the food assistance is making an impact. “As more sick die, some people are realizing what I say is true,” she says. “And they’re beginning to send suspected family members to the hospital.” WFP's Ebola response in the DRC is made possible thanks to support from Canada, Japan and the United States of America. Learn more about WFP's work supporting people in DRC affected by Ebola and hunger. Now is the time to act WFP relies entirely on voluntary contributions, so every donation counts. View five news elements
Time
The outbreak was confirmed in Mongbwalu last May and is ongoing as of November 2026. The publication date is 2026-11-03.
Location and population
The Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), specifically the mining town of Mongbwalu and eight Congolese provinces, including 66 health zones. The affected population includes over 26 million people facing severe hunger, with 3 million in Ebola-affected areas.
Event
An Ebola outbreak caused by the Bundibugyo Ebola virus strain, confirmed in Mongbwalu last May. The outbreak has resulted in over 10,000 cases and approximately 5,000 deaths. The event is confirmed by the DRC's health authorities and reported by the UN World Food Programme (WFP).
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak has sickened more than 10,000 people, killed roughly half of them, and spread to eight provinces and 66 health zones. It is described as the world’s second largest on record. The impact includes severe hunger, restricted access, and ongoing conflict in eastern DRC. The risk is not explicitly stated, but the outbreak is noted as a significant public health threat.
Response, status and uncertainty
The WFP is providing food assistance, building Ebola treatment centres, and supporting the broader humanitarian response. The outbreak is ongoing, and the response is being hampered by conflict, limited aid, restricted access, and mistrust. The situation remains under investigation and requires continued efforts.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

美国医生在刚果民主共和国检测出埃博拉病毒呈阳性 暴发 - ABC News - 突发新闻、最新新闻和视频

American doctor tests positive for Ebola in Democratic Republic of Congo outbreak - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos
新闻简报据国际基督教宣教组织塞尔日和美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 称,一名在刚果民主共和国工作的美国医生在 暴发 疫情期间检测出埃博拉病毒呈阳性。医生彼得·斯塔福德 (Peter Stafford) 在 Nyankunde 医院治疗患者时被感染,并在周末出现症状,周日晚间检测结果呈阳性。疾病预防控制中心确认了该病例,但没有透露具体人名,并表示美国公众面临的风险仍然很低。该患者和六名高危接触者正在被转移到德国接受治疗。疾病预防控制中心已启动紧急行动中心,并正在部署技术专家来管理局势。过去 21 天内去过刚果民主共和国、乌干达或南苏丹的美国护照持有者可能会面临入境限制。这一事件凸显了在资源有限的环境下管理埃博拉 暴发 所面临的持续挑战以及国际社会为降低风险而采取的应对措施。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该事件于 2026 年 11 月 3 日报道,患者在周末出现症状,并于周日晚些时候检测呈阳性。
地点与对象
该事件发生在刚果民主共和国,特别是在 Nyankunde 医院,涉及一名在那里工作的美国医生。
事件
国际基督教宣教组织 Serge 和美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 证实,一名美国医生在刚果民主共和国 暴发 期间检测出埃博拉病毒呈阳性。
规模、影响与风险
至少一名在刚​​果民主共和国工作的美国人检测出埃博拉病毒呈阳性,六名高危接触者被转移到德国接受治疗。疾病预防控制中心表示,美国公众面临的风险仍然很低。
应对、状态与不确定性
疾病预防控制中心已启动紧急行动中心并正在部署技术专家。患者和接触者正在被转移接受护理,并且对过去 21 天内去过刚果民主共和国、乌干达或南苏丹的美国护照持有者实行入境限制。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

American doctor tests positive for Ebola in Democratic Republic of Congo outbreak - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos

News Brief
An American doctor working in the Democratic Republic of the Congo has tested positive for Ebola amid an ongoing outbreak, according to the international Christian missions organization Serge and the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). The doctor, Peter Stafford, was exposed while treating patients at Nyankunde Hospital and developed symptoms over the weekend, testing positive late Sunday. The CDC confirmed the case but did not identify the individual, stating that the risk to the U.S. general public remains low. The patient and six high-risk contacts are being relocated to Germany for care. The CDC has activated its Emergency Operations Center and is deploying technical experts to manage the situation. U.S. passport holders who have been in the DRC, Uganda, or South Sudan in the past 21 days may face entry restrictions. This incident highlights the ongoing challenges of managing Ebola outbreaks in resource-limited settings and the international response to mitigate risks.

ByYouri Benadjaoud, Jack Moore, Eric M. Strauss, David Brennan, and Mary Kekatos May 18, 2026, 10:40 PM Share Follow ABC News on Google An American doctor working in the Democratic Republic of the Congo has tested positive for Ebola amid the outbreak, according to an international Christian missions organization. Dr. Peter Stafford, a medical missionary with the missions organization Serge, was exposed while treating patients at Nyankunde Hospital, the group said Monday. He sought testing "after presenting symptoms consistent with the virus," Serge said in a statement. Serge, an international Christian missions organization, confirmed today that medical missionary Dr. Peter Stafford, pictured with his wife Rebeka, has tested positive for the Bundibugyo ebolavirus variant in Bunia, Democratic Republic of Congo. Serge Stafford has served at Nyankunde Hospital with Serge since 2023, according to the organization. Two other Serge medical missionaries, including Stafford's wife, were treating patients in the region when the outbreak began and remain asymptomatic, Serge said Monday. "Our medical teams labor in some of the most demanding settings in the world, serving vulnerable communities who have limited access to healthcare," Joel Hylton, Serge's senior director of mission, said in a statement. "We are profoundly grateful for their dedication to the people of the DRC, and we deeply lament the hardship they are enduring under this current threat. Our concern extends equally to our Congolese colleagues and friends in the region who face these same risks." The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) confirmed Monday that at least one American working in the Democratic Republic of the Congo has tested positive for Ebola, but did not identify that person. Dr. Satish K. Pillai, incident manager for the CDC's Ebola response, told reporters that the individual developed symptoms over the weekend and tested positive late Sunday. Pillai added that the patient and six other high-risk contacts are being moved to Germany for care and stressed that the risk to the U.S. general public remains low. Related Ebola outbreak in Africa is 'public health emergency of international concern,' WHO says "Given the previous experience for caring for Ebola patients, coupled with the flight times being significantly shorter, this allows us to get these persons to points of care quickly," Pillai said. Pillai said the CDC has activated its Emergency Operations Center through its country offices in the DRC and in Uganda, and is deploying technical experts that have been requested from Atlanta headquarters. The CDC said earlier Monday that it is preparing to restrict entry for travelers arriving from parts of central Africa where an Ebola outbreak has been declared, in coordination with the Department of Homeland Security. Non-U.S. passport holders will face entry restrictions if they have been in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Uganda or South Sudan in the previous 21 days. The move is being carried out under Title 42 of the Public Health Services Act, which allows the CDC director to suspend entry of individuals into the U.S. to protect public health. A staff member of the CBCA Virunga Hospital checks a visitor's temperature using a a contactless infrared thermometer, in Goma, Democratic Republic of Congo, on May 17, 2026. Jospin Mwisha/AFP via Getty Images The order will be in effect for 30 days and does not apply to U.S. citizens or lawful permanent residents. Popular Reads Learjet with 9 aboard crashes in Palm Beach County, Florida: FAA Nov 1 Captain identified after deadly Florida Learjet crash 5 hours ago 2 college students found dead after going missing while hiking, police say Oct 29 The CDC said it is also coordinating with airlines, international partners and port-of-entry officials to identify and manage travelers with possible Ebola exposure as well as enhancing measures like contact tracing, laboratory testing capacity and hospital readiness nationwide. On Sunday, the CDC said in a statement that a "small number of Americans" are directly affected by an Ebola outbreak occurring in the Democratic Republic of Congo. "The CDC is working with other U.S. agencies to coordinate the safe withdrawal of the Americans," the CDC said. The agency did not confirm the number of people affected, the type of exposure or whether any individuals had experienced symptoms. "We don't discuss or comment on individual dispositions," Pillai said Sunday. "It is a highly dynamic situation, and at this point, what I would say is, we continue to assess, we will continue to keep you posted as we learn more." The State Department said Monday that its officials are working with the U.S. military and the CDC on "potential repatriation of affected Americans, based on assessed exposure and health needs." U.S. embassies in the Democratic Republic of Congo, Rwanda, South Sudan and Uganda have started "monitoring groups" to track developments and communicate with Americans in the impacted areas, the State Department said. An initial $13 million in foreign aid has also been mobilized for "immediate response efforts," including "supporting surveillance, laboratory capacity, risk communication, safe burials, entry and exit screening, and clinical case management," it said. Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. told ABC News on Monday that his agency is "working on" the Ebola outbreak. Related Everything you need to know about Ebola On Saturday, the World Health Organization said in a statement that the Ebola outbreak in the DRC and Uganda constituted a "public health emergency of international concern." As of Sunday, there were 10 confirmed Ebola cases and 336 suspected cases in the DRC. There had been 88 suspected deaths in the DRC, as well as two confirmed cases and one confirmed death in Uganda from people who had traveled to the DRC. Ebola virus spreads through direct contact with the bodily fluids of an infected person and does not spread through casual contact or air. "CDC has extensive experience and expertise in responding to Ebola outbreaks," CDC acting director Dr. Jay Bhattacharya said on a call with reporters on Friday. "It is a large outbreak, and we were just informed yesterday about it." He added, "We're absolutely committed to making sure that they can get resources as they need. We have helped with other Ebola outbreaks in the past ... we have lots of hard-earned lessons. The key thing here is to know that we are absolutely involved." This is the DRC's 17th outbreak of Ebola since the disease emerged in the 1970s, according to the WHO. This strain of Ebola is caused by Bundibugyo virus, for which there are no therapeutics or vaccines, the WHO said. The WHO has declared international public health emergencies over previous Ebola outbreaks, as well as COVID-19 and mpox. 刚果埃博拉死亡人数突破 5,000 人,担心埃博拉病毒会在整个非洲大陆蔓延

Congo’s Ebola deaths top 5,000 as fear of continent-wide spread mounts
新闻简报刚果最新的埃博拉病毒 暴发 由本迪布焦病毒引起,截至 2026 年 10 月 31 日,已造成 5,000 多人死亡,已达到悲剧性的里程碑。5 月中旬宣布的 暴发 已感染 10,351 人,致死率接近 50%。这是继2014-2016年西非暴发之后历史上第二严重的死亡事件。该病毒已传播到八个省的 67 个卫生区,其中伊图里和北基伍省为震中。尽管做出了努力,当局在遏制方面仍面临挑战,包括资源有限、社区抵制和对卫生工作者的袭击。接触者追踪和测试仍然很困难,80% 的新病例源自不受监控的 传播 连锁店。三分之一的死亡发生在 治疗 中心之外,这加剧了传播。卫生工作者也因拖欠工资而举行罢工,使应对工作进一步复杂化。
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发自2026年5月中旬以来一直持续进行,最新政府数据于2026年10月31日发布。
地点与对象
暴发 集中在刚果东部地区,特别是伊图里省和北基伍省,影响了八个省 178 个卫生区中的 67 个。
事件
刚果最新的埃博拉病毒 暴发 由本迪布焦病毒引起,已导致 10,351 例确诊病例中 5,026 人死亡,人们对整个非洲大陆的传播和遏制工作感到担忧。
规模、影响与风险
暴发的致死率接近50%,死亡人数为5,026人,感染人数为10,351人。本迪布焦病毒没有已知的 疫苗 或 治疗,80% 的新病例来自外部监控的接触者。三分之一的死亡发生在 治疗 中心之外,导致社区 传播。
应对、状态与不确定性
当局正在努力追踪和检测接触者,医护人员也面临罢工和袭击。目前情况仍在调查中,暴发 的管理和解决资源短缺问题仍面临挑战。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Congo’s Ebola deaths top 5,000 as fear of continent-wide spread mounts

News Brief
The latest Ebola outbreak in Congo, caused by the Bundibugyo virus, has reached a tragic milestone with over 5,000 deaths reported as of October 31, 2026. The outbreak, declared in mid-May, has infected 10,351 people, resulting in a nearly 50% lethality rate. It is the second deadliest in history, following the 2014-2016 West Africa outbreak. The virus has spread to 67 health zones in eight provinces, with Ituri and North Kivu as epicenters. Despite efforts, authorities face challenges in containment, including limited resources, community resistance, and attacks on health workers. Contact tracing and testing remain difficult, with 80% of new cases originating from unmonitored transmission chains. One-third of deaths occur outside treatment centers, exacerbating the spread. Health workers have also gone on strike due to unpaid wages, further complicating response efforts.
Original source text (English)

Listen to this article with a free profile 00:0000:00 A medical worker checks an infected patient at an Ebola treatment center in Bunia, Congo earlier this month. Xinhua News Agency / via Getty Share Add NBC News to Google Oct. 31, 2026, 8:16 AM EDT / Source: The Associated Press By The Associated Press Deaths in Congo’s Ebola outbreak have topped 5,000 as the country grapples with the relentless spread of the disease and cases are recorded in other African countries, the latest government data on Saturday shows. Data from the Congolese Ministry of Health showed that the disease has killed 5,026 people out of 10,351 infected, at a nearly 50% lethality rate. The surge is sparking concerns among experts regarding the authorities’ ability to contain the deadly virus. The latest outbreak, caused by the Bundibugyo virus, was declared in mid-May, catching authorities by surprise. They have since struggled to manage it. It is already the second deadliest Ebola outbreak in history, second only to a two-year-long West Africa outbreak between 2014 and 2016, which killed 11,000 people across multiple countries. “It is a very tragic milestone because it is just not a number,” said Dr. Patrick Otim, a program area manager with the World Health Organization. “It is families and communities that have been devastated by this outbreak. The number also shows the severity of this outbreak.” 2,788 people have recovered from the virus so far. The Bundibugyo virus has no known vaccine or treatment, but health workers treat patients based on symptoms. Since May, the disease has spread to 67 out of the country’s 178 health zones in eight of 26 provinces. Fifty of the affected 67 health zones have active cases. The virus has been concentrated in the eastern region of Congo, especially in Ituri and North Kivu, which have emerged as the epicenters with 9,782 total confirmed cases and 4,792 total deaths. Authorities are still struggling with tracing contacts and testing, key components in the race to curb the outbreak. According to the Africa Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, about 80% of new cases are coming from outside the chain of monitored contacts, meaning an overwhelming share of infections are coming from unknown chains of transmission to health authorities. Also, one-third of recorded deaths are victims who have died outside of treatment centers, before being tested for Ebola, WHO’s Otim said. These issues, health officials say, contribute to community transmission outside of the immediate purview of health workers who can effectively manage infection in accordance with medical protocols. “These are people we never had opportunity to try to save them and it is one of the biggest concerns for us in this outbreak. How do we try to reach people early to get them to come to health facilities to get a fighting chance to survive?” Otim said. The explosive number of infections in Congo comes on the heels of a long list of issues that have hamstrung efforts to contain the outbreak. The eastern region of Congo, where the outbreak is concentrated, plays host to a conflict between the government and an array of armed groups, most prominently the Rwanda-backed M23 rebel group, and there is also an Islamist insurgency in the border areas with Uganda, where the Allied Democratic Forces carry out attacks, limiting the reach of healthcare workers. Also, there is widespread disbelief among the population, which has led to a series of attacks on health workers and health facilities. A nurse was killed while responding to an Ebola alert last week, and a politician from the ruling party was killed in September after advocating for health measures on the radio. Health workers have gone on multiple strikes, shutting down facilities where they treat infected people, citing long delays in payments by the government. On Friday, workers at an Ebola treatment center in Tchomia, in Ituri, downed tools, demanding four months of unpaid wages. At the center, there are currently five confirmed Ebola patients and 10 suspected cases under care. “We are tired of requesting our salaries. If they want to pay us, they will. ... Today, being able to feed our families has become a real problem,” Moise Mugenyi, one of the striking workers, told the AP. Dr. Justin Mukonkole, acting head of response operations in Ituri, said the workers should be patient while the government responds to their demands. Similar situations have played out in other health zones like Beni in North Kivu, where services in some treatment centers have ground to a halt. Alain Siwako, an elected public official in Beni, said cases are spiralling in Beni because of a lack of fuel and financial resources from the central government. “Across Ituri and North Kivu, this outbreak is stretching everyone responding to it, including the communities themselves,” Rose Tchwenko, Country Director for the DRC, Prosper Global (formerly Mercy Corps), said. In October, both Kenya and Nigeria confirmed cases of Ebola. Kenya has confirmed eight cases and 2 deaths, while Nigeria’s sole confirmed case had been evacuated to Switzerland. The confirmation of cases in both countries, and earlier in Uganda, made the latest outbreak the first recorded in three African regions and the continent’s most geographically widespread outbreak. Event

Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak has a nearly 50% lethality rate, with 5,026 deaths and 10,351 infections. The Bundibugyo virus has no known vaccine or treatment, and 80% of new cases are from outside monitored contacts. One-third of deaths occur outside treatment centers, contributing to community transmission.
Response, status and uncertainty
Authorities are struggling with contact tracing and testing, and healthcare workers have faced strikes and attacks. The situation remains under investigation, with ongoing challenges in managing the outbreak and addressing resource shortages.

刚果埃博拉死亡人数已突破 5,000 人,担心在整个非洲大陆蔓延 - PBS

Congo's Ebola deaths top 5,000 as fear of continent-wide spread mounts - PBS
新闻简报根据最新的政府数据,截至 2026 年 10 月 31 日,刚果民主共和国由本迪布焦病毒引起的埃博拉 暴发 已导致 5,000 多人死亡。2026年5月中旬宣布的暴发已感染10,351人,致死率接近50%。该病毒已传播到八个省的 67 个卫生区,目前有 50 个报告活跃病例。本迪布焦病毒没有已知的 疫苗 或 治疗,三分之一的死亡发生在 治疗 中心之外。卫生当局正在努力进行接触者追踪和检测,因为 80% 的新病例来自不受监控的 传播 连锁店。暴发 引发了对整个非洲大陆蔓延的担忧,东部地区持续不断的冲突进一步加剧了这种担忧,这些冲突限制了医疗服务的获取并导致卫生工作者和设施遭到袭击。目前情况仍在调查中,我们正在努力控制疫情蔓延并提高应对能力。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026年10月31日。事件日期:暴发于2026年5月中旬宣布。此后暴发一直持续进行,最新数据显示截至2026年10月31日星期六的情况。
地点与对象
国家:刚果民主共和国(DRC)。地区:刚果民主共和国东部,特别是伊图里省和北基伍省。受影响人口:10,351 名感染者,5,026 人死亡,2,788 人康复。该病毒已传播到八个省份 178 个卫生区中的 67 个。
事件
由本迪布焦病毒引起的埃博拉 暴发 已导致刚果民主共和国 5,000 多人死亡。暴发 于 2026 年 5 月中旬宣布,并迅速传播,其他非洲国家也出现了病例。刚果卫生部报告了截至 2026 年 10 月 31 日星期六的这些数字。
规模、影响与风险
暴发已感染10,351人,造成5,026人死亡,致死率接近50%。本迪布焦病毒没有已知的 疫苗 或 治疗,三分之一的死亡发生在 治疗 中心之外。该病毒已传播到 67 个卫生区,其中 50 个存在活跃病例。这种情况引发了人们对病毒在整个非洲大陆传播的可能性的担忧。
应对、状态与不确定性
当局正在努力进行接触者追踪和测试,这对于遏制 暴发 至关重要。非洲疾病控制和预防中心指出,80%的新病例来自受监控的接触链之外。由于医疗保健服务有限和对 暴发 的普遍怀疑,社区 传播 的出现。目前情况仍在调查中,并正在努力控制传播。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Congo's Ebola deaths top 5,000 as fear of continent-wide spread mounts - PBS

News Brief
An Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, caused by the Bundibugyo virus, has resulted in over 5,000 deaths as of October 31, 2026, according to the latest government data. The outbreak, declared in mid-May 2026, has infected 10,351 people, with a nearly 50% lethality rate. The virus has spread to 67 health zones across eight provinces, with 50 currently reporting active cases. The Bundibugyo virus has no known vaccine or treatment, and one-third of deaths occurred outside of treatment centers. Health authorities are struggling with contact tracing and testing, as 80% of new cases are from unmonitored transmission chains. The outbreak has raised fears of continent-wide spread, exacerbated by ongoing conflicts in the eastern region, which limit healthcare access and have led to attacks on health workers and facilities. The situation remains under investigation, with efforts ongoing to manage the spread and improve response capabilities.

One-third of people who have died did not get to receive any care Since May, the disease has spread to 67 out of the country's 178 health zones in eight of 26 provinces. Fifty of the affected 67 health zones have active cases. The virus has been concentrated in the eastern region of Congo, especially in Ituri and North Kivu, which have emerged as the epicenters with 9,782 total confirmed cases and 4,792 total deaths. Authorities are still struggling with tracing contacts and testing, key components in the race to curb the outbreak. READ MORE: Nigeria confirms Ebola case in its capital as woman who arrived from Uganda tests positive According to the Africa Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, about 80% of new cases are coming from outside the chain of monitored contacts, meaning an overwhelming share of infections are coming from unknown chains of transmission to health authorities. Also, one-third of recorded deaths are victims who have died outside of treatment centers, before being tested for Ebola, WHO's Otim said. These issues, health officials say, contribute to community transmission outside of the immediate purview of health workers who can effectively manage infection in accordance with medical protocols. "These are people we never had opportunity to try to save them and it is one of the biggest concerns for us in this outbreak. How do we try to reach people early to get them to come to health facilities to get a fighting chance to survive?" Otim said. 'The outbreak is stretching everyone responding to it' The explosive number of infections in Congo comes on the heels of a long list of issues that have hamstrung efforts to contain the outbreak. The eastern region of Congo, where the outbreak is concentrated, plays host to a conflict between the government and an array of armed groups, most prominently the Rwanda-backed M23 rebel group, and there is also an Islamist insurgency in the border areas with Uganda, where the Allied Democratic Forces carry out attacks, limiting the reach of healthcare workers. Also, there is widespread disbelief among the population, which has led to a series of attacks on health workers and health facilities. A nurse was killed while responding to an Ebola alert last week, and a politician from the ruling party was killed in September after advocating for health measures on the radio. Health workers have gone on multiple strikes, shutting down facilities where they treat infected people, citing long delays in payments by the government. On Friday, workers at an Ebola treatment center in Tchomia, in Ituri, downed tools, demanding four months of unpaid wages. At the center, there are currently five confirmed Ebola patients and 10 suspected cases under care. READ MORE: Congo's confirmed Ebola cases surpass 9,000 as containment efforts falter "We are tired of requesting our salaries. If they want to pay us, they will. ... Today, being able to feed our families has become a real problem," Moise Mugenyi, one of the striking workers, told the AP. Dr. Justin Mukonkole, acting head of response operations in Ituri, said the workers should be patient while the government responds to their demands. Similar situations have played out in other health zones like Beni in North Kivu, where services in some treatment centers have ground to a halt. Alain Siwako, an elected public official in Beni, said cases are spiralling in Beni because of a lack of fuel and financial resources from the central government. "Across Ituri and North Kivu, this outbreak is stretching everyone responding to it, including the communities themselves," Rose Tchwenko, Country Director for the DRC, Prosper Global (formerly Mercy Corps), said. In October, both Kenya and Nigeria confirmed cases of Ebola. Kenya has confirmed eight cases and 2 deaths, while Nigeria's sole confirmed case had been evacuated to Switzerland. The confirmation of cases in both countries, and earlier in Uganda, made the latest outbreak the first recorded in three African regions and the continent's most geographically widespread outbreak. Ope Adetayo reported from Lagos, Nigeria. The Associated Press receives financial support for global health and development coverage in Africa from the Gates Foundation. Event
An Ebola outbreak caused by the Bundibugyo virus has resulted in over 5,000 deaths in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. The outbreak was declared in mid-May 2026 and has been spreading rapidly, with cases recorded in other African countries. The Congolese Ministry of Health reported these figures as of Saturday, 2026-10-31.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak has infected 10,351 people and caused 5,026 deaths, resulting in a nearly 50% lethality rate. The Bundibugyo virus has no known vaccine or treatment, and one-third of deaths occurred outside of treatment centers. The virus has spread to 67 health zones, with 50 having active cases. The situation is raising concerns about the potential for continent-wide spread.
Response, status and uncertainty
Authorities are struggling with contact tracing and testing, which are critical to containing the outbreak. The Africa Centers for Disease Control and Prevention noted that 80% of new cases are from outside the monitored contact chain. Community transmission is occurring due to limited healthcare access and widespread disbelief in the outbreak. The situation remains under investigation, with ongoing efforts to manage the spread.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola cases, AI math breakthrough and the dangers of making daylight saving time permanent - Scientific American

News Brief
The article discusses an ongoing Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), which is attributed to the Bundibugyo virus, a less common species compared to the Zaire virus that caused the West African epidemic. The outbreak, declared less than 200 days ago, has seen over 10,000 confirmed cases, with nearly half resulting in deaths. The DRC's health system is struggling to track cases and provide care, with 80% of new cases emerging from unknown sources. Distrust of health workers and government is also a challenge, as some people believe the outbreak is a hoax. Clinical trials for a vaccine candidate are underway, with the first participant vaccinated last Monday. The trial includes individuals who have had close contact with confirmed Bundibugyo virus cases. While the initial vaccine tested is for the Ebola virus, several Bundibugyo-specific vaccines are in development. The article also touches on AI advancements in solving major math problems, but this is not directly related to the Ebola outbreak.
Original source text (English)
Ebola cases, AI math breakthrough and the dangers of making daylight saving time permanent Ebola outbreak crosses grim milestone, artificial intelligence solves major math problems, and scientists caution against making daylight saving time permanent By Rachel Feltman , Peter Hall , Sushmita Pathak & Alex Sugiura Happy Monday, listeners! And happy November. For Scientific American ’s Science Quickly, I’m Rachel Feltman. You’re listening to our weekly science news roundup. We’ll start with an update on the ongoing Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. Last Tuesday, data from the country’s Ministry of Health confirmed more than 10,000 cases since May. And nearly half of those people have died. This is the fastest known Ebola outbreak in history. For context, the deadliest outbreak on record, which occurred in West Africa between 2014 and 2016, resulted in 28,000 cases and 11,000 deaths. But that outbreak lasted more than two years, and the current outbreak in the DRC was declared less than 200 days ago. And the difference in their trajectories is striking. On day 187 of the current outbreak, the official case count was 10,060. On day 186 of the West Africa outbreak, just 544 cases had been recorded. There are a few factors making this outbreak tough to manage. The first is the actual species of Ebola-causing virus fueling the outbreak. Of the six known species in this genus, four cause the kind of severe hemorrhagic fever you probably think of when you hear the word “Ebola.” The West African epidemic was caused by one of those four viruses, which is somewhat confusingly now called the Ebola virus but used to be called the Zaire virus, and which has the highest fatality rate of the four. Fortunately, an extremely effective vaccine was developed and deployed to help end that epidemic. This latest outbreak is caused by a different species: the Bundibugyo virus. It’s much less common, having only caused two other known outbreaks in the past. Another factor in the outbreak’s breakneck growth is that the DRC is really struggling to track cases and administer care. The Associated Press reports that at least half of all deaths have been in people who were never admitted to treatment centers. And contact tracing isn’t going well, either. An outbreak is considered under control when 95% of all new cases come from contact with previously known and recorded cases. But at present, something like 80% of new cases are emerging from unknown sources. In other words, there are still lots of people out transmitting the disease that haven’t yet been identified by contact tracers. Distrust of the government and, by extension, of health workers is also a huge issue, with many reportedly believing the outbreak is a hoax . Last month a nurse was killed and another health care worker was severely injured as they attempted to respond to reports of Ebola. However there is some good news, which is that clinical trials are ongoing to find a good vaccine candidate. Last Monday the first participant was vaccinated. The clinical trial is called Tumaini, which means “hope” in Swahili. The trial is made up of people who have recently been in close contact with individuals now confirmed to have the Bundibugyo virus. The first vaccine up for testing is the one tailored to prevent the Ebola virus, but several Bundibugyo-specific inoculations are currently in development and will be tested as well. Now let’s get into some news about artificial intelligence. Just a few weeks after dropping hundreds of AI-generated math breakthroughs on a field that’s already in crisis, OpenAI is piling on more. Here to tell us more about this shake-up is artificial intelligence and technology reporter Peter Hall. He’s currently working as an editorial fellow at Scientific American . Thanks so much for coming on to chat, Peter. Peter Hall: Good to meet you and good to talk with you. Feltman: So could you give our listeners a little bit of backstory for how this has been heating up? Hall: Yeah. So over the past few months OpenAI and Anthropic, as well as other players, have really been trying to flex the ability of their models by, um, proving or disproving famous math conjectures. And last week OpenAI introduced these three results across math, physics and theoretical computer science that are quite big. And Anthropic also released the same result, almost at the same time on the same day. Feltman: And so, to give our listeners a little bit of context, what kinds of problems are we talking about and what does this process usually look like, you know, when human mathematicians are involved? Hall: So there’s a huge collection of problems that mathematicians have conjectured or guessed over decades of research. For the proofs from last week, these centered around problems related to Millennium Prize Problems, which are these group of seven problems that the Clay Mathematics Institute published in 2000 as a collection of problems that have existed across math and physics and computer science that are so noteworthy and so important and so difficult that they would get a million-dollar prize for a solution to them. Feltman: So is there any controversy about whether what OpenAI and Anthropic are presenting are actually solutions to these problems, that they’re solved? Hall: Right. So that’s the other side of the coin is that, usually, you know, math research is published in journals or on preprint servers or in conferences. It’s discussed among mathematicians. These don’t really follow the same blueprint there. These are just kind of, like, put in GitHub repos as just a collection of AI-generated text and programming that they claim not only shows that the result is true but also has verified it in a programming language called Lean, which is meant specifically for mathematics. So definitely the aspect of, like, how intelligible these results are and what kind of insights people are gaining from them has been a huge point of controversy—and you know, who gets the credit when a model is just outputting a bunch of ideas that we don’t really know exactly what they’re building off of. Feltman: It’s like when that monkey took a selfie, except I had more respect for both the monkey and the photographer than I unfortunately do for a lot of AI companies. Well, what are the potential benefits to the mathematics community of these proofs being out there? You know, are there any upsides? Hall: You know with previous drops, there has been efforts from the communities to go through the proof, really, you know, with a very fine-tooth comb and turn it into something that is expository and something that we can look at the techniques for and really understand some sort of mathematical insight for it. You know, the hope is that solutions like these and the proof steps, even if they’re not written the best right now, can at some point down the line be turned into something that is useful. Like, you know, I can use the ideas from this to solve some other problem that I’ve seen elsewhere in my research. Feltman : Well thank you so much for coming on to talk through this with us. Feltman : Now for some research on an extremely timely subject: daylight saving time. DST just ended for those of us in the United States—not counting several territories, including Puerto Rico, and the exempt states Hawaii and Arizona,minus the Navajo Nation, which does still observe DST. Complicated, right? And it’s not like “springing forward” and “falling back” are much fun to deal with, either. Research suggests that shifting our clocks back and forth by an hour takes a toll on the body and the mind . So it’s not surprising that this practice is pretty unpopular, and you might have celebrated when the House of Representatives passed the Sunshine Protection Act in July. That bill, which has yet to clear the Senate, would make DST permanent and therefore “preserve” an hour of afternoon sunlight as we head deeper into fall. Last week President Donald Trump blasted the Senate for failing to take a vote in time to implement permanent DST this year, claiming in a social media post that literally no one wanted to change their clocks. While that’s characteristically hyperbolic, recent survey data does suggest that almost half of U.S. adults are opposed to all the falling and springing. But according to a new analysis published in Current Biology , making DST permanent could have serious, dangerous consequences for school-age children. Researchers at the University of Washington who study how school start times impact adolescent health decided to quantify exactly how many more trips to school would happen in darkness if we switched to year-round DST. They found that the Sunshine Protection Act would lead to more than two billion dark morning commutes for children. They also confirmed that fatal morning crashes involving kids aged five to 18 are more likely to occur if the sun has yet to rise. So those additional dark commutes really do pose a risk. The researchers aren’t arguing in favor of continuing to swap our clocks back and forth; in fact, they point out that while permanent DST would be the worst-case scenario for dark school commutes, our current system creates twice as many dark mornings as would occur under permanent standard time. Listen: I’m as bummed out by an afternoon sunset as anyone else. But as long as we can get rid of the twice-a-year upheaval, I’m personally good with whatever . And keeping kids safe as they wait for the bus—or, even better, as they walk or bike to school, because we love a pedestrian-friendly commute—seems pretty worthwhile to me. That’s all for this week’s science news roundup. We’ll be back on Wednesday to talk about how soybeans quietly swept the nation and on Friday we’re going to talk about how AI swept everywhere. Ebola in Nigeria: No suspected case for northeast afta laboratory tests show negative - NCDC - BBC
新闻简报尼日利亚疾病控制与预防中心(NCDC)证实,尼日利亚阿达马瓦州两例埃博拉疑似病例的实验室检测结果呈阴性,表明没有阳性病例。疑似病例在东北地区首府约拉发现,已被隔离以进一步调查。国家疾控中心强调,这些还不是确诊病例,公众应保持警惕,保持良好的卫生习惯,如果出现发烧、虚弱或出血等症状,请立即就医。该机构还指出,在一名埃博拉阳性患者从尼日利亚疏散至瑞士后,这两个人是管理潜在 暴发 的更广泛努力的一部分。继 2026 年 10 月 27 日初步报告疑似病例后,结果于 2026 年 10 月 31 日发布。国家疾病预防控制中心重申,尼日利亚唯一确诊病例仍在联邦首都特区 (FCT)。

时间
发布日期:2026年10月31日。事件日期:2026年10月27日。检测结果:2026年10月31日。疑似病例报告于2026年10月27日,实验室结果于2026年10月31日发布。
地点与对象
尼日利亚,特别是东北地区的阿达马瓦州。受影响人群包括阿达马瓦州首府约拉的两名出现埃博拉症状的人。
事件
尼日利亚疾病控制与预防中心(NCDC)宣布,阿达马瓦州两例埃博拉疑似病例的实验室检测结果为阴性,确认没有阳性病例。NCDC 报道了该事件,并在 BBC 文章中引用。
规模、影响与风险
阿达马瓦州没有确诊的埃博拉病例。两名疑似病例已被隔离并正在调查中。国家疾病预防控制中心强调,公众应保持良好的卫生习惯,如果怀疑接触过病毒,请报告症状。在进一步测试确认病例状态之前,风险仍然很低。

国家疾病预防控制中心和阿达马瓦州当局正在进行接触者追踪和实验室检测以确认病例。埃博拉病毒 的结果是否定的,但调查仍在进行中。来源是一篇完整的文章,因此内容没有不确定性。
